=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given permutations P = (P_1, P_2, \ldots, P_N) and A = (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N) of (1,2,\ldots,N).
You can perform the following operation any number of times, possibly zero:

- replace A_i with A_{P_i} simultaneously for all i=1,2,\ldots,N.

Print the lexicographically smallest A that can be obtained.
What is lexicographical order?
 For sequences of length N, A = (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N) and B = (B_1, B_2, \ldots, B_N), A is lexicographically smaller than B if and only if:

- there exists an integer i\ (1\leq i\leq N) such that A_i < B_i, and A_j = B_j for all 1\leq j < i.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
P_1 P_2 \ldots P_N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Let (A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N) be the lexicographically smallest A that can be obtained. Print A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N in this order, separated by spaces, in one line.

Constraints


- 1\leq N\leq2\times10^5
- 1\leq P_i\leq N\ (1\leq i\leq N)
- P_i\neq P_j\ (1\leq i<j\leq N)
- 1\leq A_i\leq N\ (1\leq i\leq N)
- A_i\neq A_j\ (1\leq i<j\leq N)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
3 1 5 6 2 4
4 3 1 6 2 5

Sample Output 1

1 4 2 5 3 6

Initially, A = (4, 3, 1, 6, 2, 5).
Repeating the operation yields the following.

- A = (1, 4, 2, 5, 3, 6)
- A = (2, 1, 3, 6, 4, 5)
- A = (3, 2, 4, 5, 1, 6)
- A = (4, 3, 1, 6, 2, 5)

After this, A will revert to the original state every four operations.
Therefore, print the lexicographically smallest among these, which is 1 4 2 5 3 6.

Sample Input 2

8
3 5 8 7 2 6 1 4
1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8

Sample Output 2

1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8

You may choose to perform no operations.

Sample Input 3

26
24 14 4 20 15 19 16 11 23 22 12 18 21 3 6 8 26 2 25 7 13 1 5 9 17 10
15 3 10 1 13 19 22 24 20 4 14 23 7 26 25 18 11 6 9 12 2 21 5 16 8 17

Sample Output 3

4 1 22 18 20 13 14 6 15 11 3 26 2 12 5 23 9 10 25 24 7 17 16 21 19 8

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now, let's scrutinize the greedy proof more formally, because final answer code only but need ensure no hidden issue.

Let S_t be set of t modulo L_total satisfying prefix positions 1..i-1 equal to lexicographically minimal prefix. Initially all t. Invariant: S_t is a residue class modulo M (i.e., t ≡ R mod M). For position i:
- If cycle of i already processed, then t mod L_i is fixed because M is multiple of L_i? Wait if cycle processed, M includes L_i? If cycle processed and L_i not dividing M? We update M to lcm(M,L_i), so yes M multiple of L_i. Thus t mod L_i fixed. So value fixed; S unchanged.
- If cycle not processed, M may or may not be multiple of L_i. If M multiple of L_i, then t mod L_i fixed; value fixed; no choice. But cycle not processed yet; we can mark processed and S unchanged. If M not multiple, d<L_i, allowed t mod L_i are y ≡ R mod d. We choose minimal A[c[y]] among allowed y. Let y0 be argmin. New S = {t∈S: t≡y0 mod L_i}, which is residue class modulo lcm(M,L_i). This is exactly lexicographic greedy. Continue. At end S nonempty because each intersection compatible by construction. The final residue gives lexicographically minimal A. Good.

Need ensure if M multiple of L_i but cycle not processed, marking processed without updating M is okay for future cycles? M already includes L_i, so yes.

Now, implementation details.

Input reading:
import sys, math
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
N = data[0]
P = [x-1 for x in data[1:1+N]]
A = [x-1 for x in data[1+N:1+2*N]]

Cycle decomposition:
visited = [False]*N
cycles = []
cycle_id = [-1]*N
offset = [0]*N
for i in range(N):
    if not visited[i]:
        cur = i
        cyc = []
        while not visited[cur]:
            visited[cur] = True
            cycle_id[cur] = len(cycles)
            offset[cur] = len(cyc)
            cyc.append(cur)
            cur = P[cur]
        # Since P is permutation, starting unvisited, we will return to i, not to some visited other. But if using while not visited, could encounter visited from previous cycle? Starting unvisited and following permutation, cannot enter previous cycle because permutation cycles disjoint. So okay.
        cycles.append(cyc)
Need be careful: If cur returns to i after L steps, visited[i] True, loop stops. Good. But if due to bug, cur could be visited not i; but permutation ensures no. We can optionally assert.

Processed = [False]*len(cycles)
M = 1
R = 0  # t ≡ R mod M

For pos in range(N):
    cid = cycle_id[pos]
    if processed[cid]:
        continue
    L = len(cycles[cid])
    # If L divides M, fixed
    # d = gcd(M, L)
    d = math.gcd(M, L)
    if d == L:
        processed[cid] = True
        continue
    # Need choose min among offsets k in 0..L-1 with k % d == R % d
    # R % d: R huge but d small. Since R < M, but R can be huge. R % d okay.
    rem = R % d
    cyc = cycles[cid]
    best_val = N+1
    best_k = -1
    # scan k from 0 to L-1; if k % d == rem
    # Could optimize by iterating k = rem; k < L; k += d, but rem < d; d maybe large. This visits L/d positions, not L. But total? If we iterate only allowed offsets, total over cycles of L/d. d can be 1 => L. d can be large => fewer. Sum ≤ N. Good. Use for k in range(rem, L, d): val = A[cyc[k]]; if val < best_val...
    # But note rem = R % d; R may be huge, but rem < d ≤ L. Good.
    for k in range(rem, L, d):
        val = A[cyc[k]]
        if val < best_val:
            best_val = val
            best_k = k
    x = best_k  # t mod L
    # Update M,R via CRT
    # M' = lcm(M,L) = M // d * L
    # Need solve (M/d) * k ≡ (x - R_mod_L)/d (mod L/d)
    # R_mod_L = R % L (small)
    R_mod_L = R % L
    l_prime = L // d
    # rhs = ((x - R_mod_L) // d) % l_prime
    rhs = ((x - R_mod_L) // d) % l_prime
    # m_prime_mod = (M // d) % l_prime. But M//d huge. Use M_mod_L = M % L, then (M_mod_L // d) % l_prime?
    # Let's verify: M_mod_L = M % L. Since L = d*l_prime. M = q*L + M_mod_L. M/d = q*l_prime + M_mod_L/d. Mod l_prime = (M_mod_L // d) mod l_prime. Because M_mod_L divisible by d? Is M_mod_L divisible by d? d divides L and d divides M, so d divides M_mod_L = M - qL, yes. Good.
    M_mod_L = M % L
    m_prime_mod = (M_mod_L // d) % l_prime
    if l_prime == 1:
        kcoef = 0
    else:
        inv = pow(m_prime_mod, -1, l_prime)
        kcoef = (inv * rhs) % l_prime
    # R' = R + M * kcoef
    R = R + M * kcoef
    M = M // d * L
    processed[cid] = True

Let's estimate worst-case M bits more carefully. Could M be product of many pairwise coprime lengths with sum N, product e^{sum log lengths}. To maximize product with fixed sum and pairwise coprime? If no pairwise coprime, lcm can be less than product. But product of lengths with fixed sum N is maximized by using 3s, but pairwise coprime prevents repeated 3. However, lcm can be as high as product of prime powers, and sum of prime powers can be N. The maximum log lcm for a set of positive integers summing N is maybe ~ sqrt(N log N)? Let's find upper bound: lcm of numbers ≤ product of numbers. Product of positive integers with sum N is maximized by 3s, log product ≤ (N/3) log 3 ≈ 0.366N bits ≈0.528N? Actually log2 product ≤ N log2(3)/3 ≈0.528N bits. So M bits ≤0.53N ~ 100k bits for N=2e5, not 300k. But can lcm approach product with many 3s? Need lengths 3 repeated not coprime, lcm=3 not product. Pairwise coprime lengths cannot repeat, so product lower. But upper bound 100k bits. But actual cycles count with large M maybe smaller. Let's think worst-case for number of cycles and M size.

d = math.gcd(M_mod_L, L)

    processed[cid] = True

rem = R_mod_L % d
cyc = cycles[cid]
best_val = N + 1
best_k = -1

    val = A[cyc[k]]
    if val < best_val:
        best_val = val
        best_k = k
# best_k should not be -1
x = best_k
l_prime = L // d
rhs = ((x - R_mod_L) // d) % l_prime
m_prime_mod = (M_mod_L // d) % l_prime
if l_prime == 1:
    kcoef = 0
else:
    inv = pow(m_prime_mod, -1, l_prime)
    kcoef = (inv * rhs) % l_prime
old_M = M
R = R + old_M * kcoef
M = old_M * l_prime
processed[cid] = True

If I implement modinv:
def modinv(a, m):
    # gcd(a,m)=1, m>1
    b=m; u=1; v=0
    while b:
        t=a//b
        a,b = b,a-t*b
        u,v = v,u-t*v
    return u % m
But need careful with variable names. Since m small, okay. But pow is built-in and faster. I think use pow. AtCoder Python 3.11. The final answer can use pow.

Let's draft code:

import sys, math

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N = data[0]
    P = [x-1 for x in data[1:1+N]]
    A = [x-1 for x in data[1+N:1+2*N]]

    visited = [False]*N
    cycle_id = [-1]*N
    offset = [0]*N
    cycles = []

    for i in range(N):
        if not visited[i]:
            cid = len(cycles)
            cur = i
            cyc = []
            while not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                cycle_id[cur] = cid
                offset[cur] = len(cyc)
                cyc.append(cur)
                cur = P[cur]
            cycles.append(cyc)

    C = len(cycles)
    processed = [False]*C
    M = 1
    R = 0

    for pos in range(N):
        cid = cycle_id[pos]
        if processed[cid]:
            continue
        L = len(cycles[cid])
        M_mod_L = M % L
        d = math.gcd(M_mod_L, L)
        if d == L:
            processed[cid] = True
            continue

        R_mod_L = R % L
        rem = R_mod_L % d
        cyc = cycles[cid]
        best_val = N + 1
        best_k = -1
        for k in range(rem, L, d):
            val = A[cyc[k]]
            if val < best_val:
                best_val = val
                best_k = k

        x = best_k
        l_prime = L // d
        rhs = ((x - R_mod_L) // d) % l_prime
        m_prime_mod = (M_mod_L // d) % l_prime
        if l_prime == 1:
            kcoef = 0
        else:
            inv = pow(m_prime_mod, -1, l_prime)
            kcoef = (inv * rhs) % l_prime
        old_M = M
        R = R + old_M * kcoef
        M = old_M * l_prime
        processed[cid] = True

    cycle_t_mod = [0]*C
    for cid, cyc in enumerate(cycles):
        L = len(cyc)
        cycle_t_mod[cid] = R % L

    ans = [0]*N
    for pos in range(N):
        cid = cycle_id[pos]
        L = len(cycles[cid])
        idx = cycles[cid][(offset[pos] + cycle_t_mod[cid]) % L]
        ans[pos] = A[idx] + 1

    sys.stdout.write(' '.join(map(str, ans)) + '\n')

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's think about if `m_prime_mod = (M_mod_L // d) % l_prime` and M_mod_L//d maybe not integer? d divides M_mod_L because d=gcd(M_mod_L,L). Yes. If M_mod_L=0 and d=L, skip; if d<L, M_mod_L>0? Could M_mod_L=0 and d=L only, so yes. Good.

We need solve:
t ≡ R (mod M)
t ≡ x (mod L)
Let d = gcd(M,L), M = d*m, L = d*l, gcd(m,l)=1.
Compatibility: x ≡ R (mod d). We choose x satisfying.
Let t = R + M*k. Need R + M*k ≡ x (mod L)
=> M*k ≡ x - R (mod L)
=> d*m*k ≡ x - R (mod d*l)
Since x-R divisible by d, divide by d:
m*k ≡ (x-R)/d (mod l)
We need m mod l. m = M/d. We compute M_mod_L = M mod L = M mod (d*l). Since d|M, M_mod_L divisible by d. M/d mod l = (M_mod_L/d) mod l. Proof: M = q*d*l + M_mod_L. Divide by d: M/d = q*l + M_mod_L/d. Mod l = M_mod_L/d mod l. Good.
RHS: (x-R)/d mod l. R_mod_L = R mod L = R mod (d*l). Since x and R_mod_L are in [0,L-1], and x ≡ R_mod_L mod d, (x - R_mod_L)/d is congruent to (x-R)/d mod l? Let's check: R = q*L + R_mod_L = q*d*l + R_mod_L. Then (x-R)/d = (x - R_mod_L)/d - q*l. So mod l same. Thus rhs = ((x - R_mod_L)//d) % l. Good.
Then k ≡ m^{-1} * rhs mod l. kcoef in [0,l-1]. R_new = R + M*k. Mod M_new = M*l, and R_new < M_new as argued. Good.
